.

Proof.

We have two tasks to accomplish. First, we will put aside our input and use
Lemma

3.4
to show how to compute the walk matrix for all of
U
; this will be fairly direct from the lemma using
\mathsf{SHORT}
as our base case. Second, we will show how to translate from connectivity across all of
U
to the actual sets
V_{in}
and
V_{out}
, as well as switching our
\tau
values from the internal memory of
Lemma

3.4
to the vectors

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

; this will be handled by looping and cancellations.

Consider
Lemma

3.4
with graph
H
, setting
C=1
and
\ell=\log|U|
. Putting aside

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

for the moment, we will use catalytic registers

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{1}\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{2}\hfil

, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{3}\hfil

as its internal vectors

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{mid}\hfil

respectively, each of length
m/C=|V_{U}|/1
.

Our base case
P_{0}
will be given by executing
\mathsf{SHORT}
with
V_{in}=V_{out}=U
and using the current input and output vectors as

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

respectively, plus auxiliary free space
S
and catalytic space
R
. By the correctness of
\mathsf{SHORT}
, this exactly adds the weight
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{0}
to

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{j}\hfil

for whichever
i,j\in\{in,out,mid\}
it is invoked on; this is the same as adding
\mu(v_{in},v_{out})
along each edge
(v_{in},v_{out})\in V_{U}^{2}
as required.

Thus
Lemma

3.4
gives us a program
P
(and its inverse
P^{-1}
) which adds
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\ell}
to

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

as required, doing so in time

(T+\mathsf{poly}\log n)\cdot(4C)^{\log|V_{U}|}\leq T\cdot\left(\frac{n}{2^{\sqrt{\log n}}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{n}{2^{\sqrt{\log n}}}\right)^{2}\cdot\mathsf{poly}\log n\leq T\cdot n^{2}

where the
\mathsf{poly}\log n
terms comes from
4.1
to run
\mathcal{U}
when calling
P_{0}
. Our space usage will be
S+\log|V_{U}|\cdot\log 4C
from
Lemma

3.4
plus
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
to compute the vertices of
U
(from
4.1
), for a total of
S+\mathcal{O}(\log n)
. Lastly our catalytic space will be
R
plus the vectors

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{1}\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{2}\hfil

, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{3}\hfil

, and by construction and the guarantee that
Lemma

4.4
gives a catalytic subroutine, all catalytic memory is reset except

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{2}\hfil

.

In order to move to our original input and output registers, we will now create interfacing between

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{1}\hfil

as well as between

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{2}\hfil

and

4.2
Short Walks via Color Classes

Our goal now is to compute
\mathsf{SHORT}
. By the definition of the weights of
H
, we have that computing
\mathcal{W}_{0}
with respect
H
is equivalent to computing
\mathcal{W}_{\log\lambda}
with respect to
G^{\prime}
plus accounting for multiplicities; thus we utilize
Lemma

3.4
with this goal in mind.

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume we have access to graph
G=(V,E)
on
n\in\mathbb{N}
vertices, prime
p=\mathsf{poly}(n)
, and seed
\sigma
. Fix

, define graphs
G^{\prime}
and
H
as per
Definition

4.2
, and define
\mathbb{F}:=\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
C:=\lambda
.

\mathsf{SHORT}(V_{in},V_{out};\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \mathsf{SHORT}
runs in time

\mathcal{O}(\log n)
, and uses catalytic memory
|V_{in}|+|V_{out}|+\frac{3n}{2^{\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log n}}}\cdot\mathcal{O}(\log p)
, namely the vectors

Proof.

Redefine
\mathcal{W}
to be with respect to
G^{\prime}
for the rest of this proof. We will do the same two-step procedure as before, first computing the walk matrix between each
(c_{in},c_{out})\in[C]^{2}
pair, and second moving the relevant answers to the input and output vectors. The former will be very straightforward from
Lemma

3.4
, while the latter mostly follows the interfacing of
Lemma

4.3
; however, we will need to additionally handle the changeover from
V_{U}
(with multiplicity) to
V^{\prime}
.

Consider
Lemma

3.4
on graph
G^{\prime}
using weight function
\mu(e)=1
, taking
C
as given and
\ell:=\log\lambda
. Again putting aside

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

for the moment, we will use catalytic registers

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{1}\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{2}\hfil

, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{3}\hfil

as its internal vectors

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{mid}\hfil

respectively, each of length
n/C
.

We define a base case
P_{0}
, which will be checking connectivity in
G^{\prime}
. Since
\ell=0
, the set
\Gamma_{0}(v_{in},v_{out})
contains the singleton edge
(v_{in},v_{out})
of weight 1 if it is in
E^{\prime}
and is empty otherwise. Thus our program does the following:

which can be computed in time
2m\log C+|E^{\prime}|\cdot\mathsf{poly}\log|\mathbb{F}|
and space
2\log(m/C)+\log C+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|)
by
Proposition

2.9
.

Applying
Lemma

3.4
, we get that for any
c_{in},c_{out}\in[C]
we have an algorithm
P(c_{in},c_{out})
(as well as its inverse) which successfully adds

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{1}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{1}\hfil$\crcr}}}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\ell}
to

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{2}\hfil

, and which takes time

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(4C)^{\ell}(2m\log C+|E^{\prime}|\cdot\mathsf{poly}\log|\mathbb{F}|)&\leq&\mathcal{O}(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(\cdot 2^{\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log m}})^{\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log m}})\\
&&\quad\cdot 2^{2\cdot\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log m}}\cdot\mathsf{poly}\log m\\
&=&\mathcal{O}(|E|\cdot n^{\epsilon/1.01})\end{array}

and uses free space

\ell\log 4C+3\log(n/C)+\log C+\mathcal{O}(|\log\mathbb{F}|)\leq(\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log n})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\log n)=\mathcal{O}(\log n)

as desired.

\mathsf{SHORT}
will directly invoke
P(c_{in},c_{out})
over each pair of color classes and ignore all answers except those from vertices
V_{in}\cap V_{c_{in}}
to vertices

(v_{in},v_{out})\in V_{in}\times V_{out}
once.

Lemma

4.3
, and so we only need confirm what happens to vertices with multiplicity. Consider a set of vertices
u_{1}\ldots u_{k}\in V_{U}
which are copies of the same vertex
v_{in}\in V^{\prime}
, and consider some
v_{out}
. Restricting our view to
(v_{in},v_{out})
and letting

as desired.
∎

4.3
Final Algorithm

Our
\mathsf{LONG}
algorithm, using
\mathsf{SHORT}
as a subroutine, gives us everything we need to compute
\mathsf{STCONN}
, with the remaining issues being to choose
U
and
p
so as to ensure correctness and efficiency.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

For the moment assume we have access to prime
p=\mathsf{poly}(n)
and seed
\sigma
. Define
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, define
\lambda:=2^{\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log n}}
, and let
U\coloneqq U_{\sigma}
, and subsequently
H=(V_{U},E_{U})
be as given in
4.1
and
Definition

4.2
respectively.

Define
V_{in}:=\{s\}
and
V_{out}:=\{t\}
, and we will have
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}},\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}\in\mathbb{F}
come from the free work tape instead of the catalytic tape, being initialized to
1
and
0
respectively. Thus running
\mathsf{LONG}
gives a final value of

0+1\cdot\sum_{W\in\Gamma_{\log|V_{U}|}(s,t)}\mu(W)

where
\Gamma
is taken over
H
.
7
7

7

Note that because each vertex in
G^{\prime}
has self-loops and hence walks of length
\lambda
to itself, the vertices of
H
also each have self-loops of the appropriate weight.
Moving to
G^{\prime}
, every walk in
G^{\prime}
has weight 1 since all edges have weight 1, and so our final value will be

\sum_{W\in\Gamma_{\log|V_{U}|}(s,t)}\mu(W)=\sum_{W\in\Gamma_{n}^{*}(s,t)}1=|\Gamma_{n}^{*}(s,t)|

where
\Gamma_{n}^{*}(s,t)
is the set of walks from
s
to
t
in
G^{\prime}
of length
n
which pass through nodes in
U
at most every
\lambda
steps.

Assuming we have our modulus
p
and our seed
\sigma
for
4.1
on the free work tape, putting
Lemma

4.3
and
Lemma

4.4
together our total runtime is

\mathcal{O}(|E|\cdot n^{\epsilon})\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{2})=\mathcal{O}(|E|\cdot n^{2+\epsilon})

our free space usage is

\mathcal{O}(\log p+\log|U|+\log n)=\mathcal{O}(\log n)

and our catalytic space usage is

6\cdot\left(\frac{n}{2^{\sqrt{(\epsilon/2.1)\log n}}}\right)\cdot\mathcal{O}(\log p)=\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}

Because
Lemma

4.3
does not change any values of the catalytic tape besides the output registers, which we have put on the free work tape, our catalytic memory is successfully returned to its original configuration.

Our deterministic algorithm simply loops over all descriptions of
p
and
\sigma
, and accepts iff any choice gives a non-zero answer, while our randomized algorithm chooses
p
and
\sigma
uniformly and thus incurs no time overhead. Clearly if there is no
s
–
t
path, no choice of
p
and
\sigma
returns a non-zero answer, and so assume otherwise. By
4.1
,
U
is such that if there is any
s
–
t
path in
G
, then
\Gamma^{*}_{n}(s,t)
will be non-empty with probability 0.99. Conversely, since every walk in
\Gamma^{*}_{n}(s,t)
is an
s
–
t
walk in
G^{\prime}
of length
n
, we have

|\Gamma^{*}_{n}(s,t)|\leq n^{n}=2^{n\log n}

since at every step there are at most
|G^{\prime}|=n
choices of the next vertex for the walk. Thus by
Proposition

2.10
,
|\Gamma_{n}^{*}(s,t)|
will be non-zero modulo
p
with probability 0.99 over a random choice of modulus
p\in[n\log^{2}n,3n\log^{2}n]
(which can be picked at random using a standard procedure of trial-and-error in time
\mathsf{poly}(\log(n))
). In total, over a random choice of
p
and
U
we have a 0.98 chance of accepting whenever
s
and
t
are connected.
∎

5
Constructing Representative Multisets for Directed Graphs

In this section we prove
4.1
. We start by recalling the statement.
See
4.1

We call multisets
U_{\sigma}
that satisfy Item (2) as a
\lambda/2
-
representative multiset
.

Our construction of representative multisets will rely on the classical and well-used notion of
expander graphs
. An undirected (multigraph)
D
is an
(N,\Delta,\alpha)
-
expander graph
if it is a
\Delta
-regular connected graph on
N
vertices, and the second-highest eigenvalue of its normalized adjacency matrix is bounded by
\alpha\in[0,1]
(in absolute value). Moreover, the family
\{D_{N}\}
is said to have a (fully)
explicit
construction, if there exists a deterministic algorithm that takes a vertex
v\in\{0,1\}^{\log(N)}
, and a number
i\in[\Delta]
as inputs (in binary), and computes the
i^{\text{th}}
neighbor of
v
in time
\mathsf{poly}(\log(N))
and space
\mathcal{O}(\log N)
. We use the following explicit expander construction.

Lemma 5.1

(
[
64
,
66
]
)
.

For every prime
p
and every positive integer
k
, there exists an infinite family of explicit graphs
\{D_{N}\}
, such that each
D_{N}
is an
(N,\Delta,\alpha)
-expander for
\Delta=p^{k}+1
and
\alpha\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\Delta-1}}{\Delta}
.

This is followed by the definition of an expander random walk.

Definition 5.2

(Expander random walk)
.

For any
(N,\Delta,\alpha)
-expander
D=(V_{D},E_{D})
, an
expander random walk

(X_{1},\dots,X_{K})
of length
K-1
, is one where
X_{1}
is the uniform distribution over
[N]
, and for every
i\in[K-1]
,
X_{i}
is the uniform distribution over the
\Delta
-sized neighborhood of the
(i-1)^{\text{th}}
-vertex in the random walk.

For any
\mathcal{B}\subset V_{D}
, its density is defined as
\frac{|\mathcal{B}|}{V_{D}}
. Then, an expander random walk of length
K
over
[N]
behaves similar to uniformly sampling
K
elements from
[N]
, in the following sense.

Lemma 5.3

(
[
7
]
(see also
[
11
]
))
.

Let
D
be an
(N,\Delta,\alpha)
-expander, and let
\mathcal{B}\subset V_{D}
of density
\beta
, for some
\beta>0
. Then, for every
K
, the probability that an expander random walk
(X_{1},\dots,X_{K})
of length
(K-1)
in
D
never leaves
\mathcal{B}
, is at most
\beta(\beta+\alpha\cdot(1-\beta))^{K-1}
.

Next, we have the following lemma about a pairwise independent hash function family
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
. We say that a hash function
h

hits
a set
B\subseteq[n]
, if there exists an
i\in[m]
, such that
h(i)\in B
, i.e., if
\mathsf{Im}(h)
intersects
B
. We prove that a randomly chosen
h
from
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
, where
m
is
\mathcal{O}(n/\lambda)
, hits any
\lambda
-sized subset of
[n]
with large probability.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any constant
0<\beta<1/2
, let
m=\frac{n(1/\beta)}{\lambda}
, and let
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
be a pairwise independent hash function family from
[m]
to
[n]
. Then, for any
B\subset[n]
of size
\lambda
we have,

\Pr_{h\sim\mathcal{H}_{m,n}}[h\text{ hits }B]\geq 1-\beta.

Proof.

Let
h
be a random hash function sampled from
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
. For every
i\in[m]
, define the indicator random variable
X_{i}
that is
1
iff
h(i)\in B
. Note that the the random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are pairwise independent from the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
.

Let
X=\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}
, be the random variable that counts the size of
\mathsf{Im}(h)\cap B
. Observe that

\text{E}[X]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\text{E}[X_{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Pr[h(i)\in B]=\frac{m\lambda}{n},

and from the pairwise independence of the
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
,

\text{Var}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\text{Var}(X_{i})=\frac{m\lambda}{n}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)=\text{E}[X]\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{n}\right).

Upper bounding the probability that
h
does not hit
B
, i.e.,
X=0
, by
\beta
, we prove the lemma.

\begin{split}\Pr_{h\sim\mathcal{H}_{m,n}}[X=0]&\leq\Pr_{h\sim\mathcal{H}_{m,n}}\left[|X-\text{E}[X]|\geq\text{E}[X]\right]\leq\frac{\text{Var}(X)}{(\text{E}[X])^{2}}\\
&=\frac{\text{E}[X]\left(1-\lambda/n\right)}{(\text{E}[X])^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\text{E}[X]}\\
&\leq\frac{n}{m\lambda}=\beta.\\
\end{split}

The second inequality follows from Chebyshev’s inequality
8
8

8

For any random variable
X
with finite non-zero variance, and for any
a>0
,
\Pr_{X}\left[|X-\text{E}[X]|\geq a\right]\leq\frac{Var(X)}{a^{2}}
.

[
73
]
, and the final probability calculation comes from our setting of
m=\frac{n(1/\beta)}{\lambda}
.
∎

We now show a probabilistic construction of representative multisets. Let
D
be an explicit
(N,D,\alpha)
-expander, where
N=\{0,1\}^{2\log(n)}
, and each vertex describes a function in
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
. Picking up the hash functions obtained from the vertices of a random walk of length
K=\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
on
D
, we prove that for every pair of vertices
u,v
connected by a path of length
\lambda/2
, at least of one of the hash functions hits some walk visiting
\lambda/2
distinct vertices between
u
and
v
, with high probability. Finally, this property implies that the union of their images is a
\lambda/2
-representative multiset, with high probability.
9
9

9

In fact, notice that the two properties are equivalent; for
u
and
v
connected by a path of length
\lambda/2
, item(2) implies item (1) trivially.

Proof of
4.1
.

Let
0<\beta<1/2
be a small constant that we set later in the proof. For a fixed
\lambda=\omega(\log(n))
and
\varepsilon>0
, let
m=\frac{2n(1/\beta)}{\lambda}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
be a pairwise independent hash function family from
[m]
to
[n]
, given by
Lemma

2.8
.

Let
D=(V_{D},E_{D})
be an
(N,\Delta,\alpha)
-expander, where
N=|\mathcal{H}_{m,n}|
, and
\Delta
is chosen such that
\alpha\leq 2\beta
. Specifically, we use the explicit construction from
Lemma

5.1
, by setting
\Delta=\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}+1
(assume that
1/\beta
is a prime power), which in turn sets
\alpha\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\Delta-1}}{\Delta}\leq 2\beta
.

For any seed
\sigma
, the multiset
U_{\sigma}
is constructed as follows: for
K=\left(2+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)\log(n)/\log\left(\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)
, use
\sigma
to specify the vertices
(X_{1},\dots,X_{K})
as a walk of length
K-1
on the expander
D
(as described in
Definition

5.2
). Interpreting each
X_{i}
as the description of a hash function
h_{i}
from
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
, the set
U_{\sigma}
is defined as
\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}\mathsf{Im}(h_{i})
, i.e., the union of the images of all
h_{i}
’s. Clearly, the size of
U_{\sigma}
is
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{n\log(n)}{\lambda}\right)
.

We next show that a seed length of
(6+\varepsilon)\log(n)
for
\sigma
, suffices to construct
U_{\sigma}
. Firstly, observe from
Lemma

2.8
that the description length of any
h\in\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
is
\log(N)=\max\{\log(m),\log(n)\}+\log(n)=2\log(n)
bits, since
m<n
for a constant
\beta
. The vertices
h_{1},\dots,h_{K}
on a walk in
D
can be specified using
\log(N)+K\cdot\log(\Delta)
, which is equal to
2\log(n)+\frac{\log(n)(2+\varepsilon/8)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)}\cdot\log\left(\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}+1\right)
. Since
\lim_{\beta\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}+1\right)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)}=2
we can set
\beta>0
to be a small enough constant so that
\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}+1\right)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)}<(2+\varepsilon/4)
. Thus, the walk on
D
can be described using at most
2\log(n)+((2+\varepsilon/8)(2+\varepsilon/4))\log(n)
bits, which is at most
(6+\varepsilon)\cdot\log(n)
, and a seed length of
(6+\varepsilon)\log(n)
is sufficient.

Next, we prove that with high probability over the choice
\sigma
(or a random walk of length
K
on
D
), for
every
pair
u,v\in V
that is connected by a path from
u
to
v
of length
\lambda/2
, there exists at least one walk
\rho_{u,v}
that visits
(\lambda/2)-1
distinct vertices, and is hit by
U_{\sigma}
(i.e., one of the hash functions that form
U_{\sigma}
).

Lemma 5.5
.

For a small enough constant
0<\beta<1/2
, let
D=(V_{D},E_{D})
be an
(N,\Delta,\alpha)
-expander, where
N=|\mathcal{H}_{m,n}|
, and
\Delta=\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}+1
.

Then, for any
G=(V,E)
, any
\varepsilon>0
, for
K=\frac{\left(2+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)\log(n)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)}
, we have

\Pr_{\sigma\sim\{0,1\}^{(6+\varepsilon)\log(n)}}\left[U_{\sigma}\text{ is a }\lambda/2\text{-representative set for }G\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n^{\varepsilon/8}}

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda/2
. Pick any
u,v\in V
, for which there exists a
\lambda^{\prime}
-length path from
u
to
v
, and fix some walk
\rho_{u,v}
that visits distinct vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{\lambda^{\prime}-1}
from
u
to
v
. Define the set
P_{u,v}=\{u,p_{1},\dots,p_{\lambda^{\prime}-1}\}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the subset of
V_{D}
in the expander graph
D
that contains hash functions in
\mathcal{H}_{m,n}
that do not hit
P_{u,v}
. Applying
Lemma

5.4
for subsets of size
\lambda^{\prime}
and
m=\frac{n(1/\beta)}{\lambda^{\prime}}
, we see that
\mathcal{B}
has density at most
\beta
. In addition, observe that from the expander construction in
Lemma

5.1
,
\alpha\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\Delta-1}}{\Delta}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\Delta}}\leq 2\beta
.

From the property of an expander random walk given by
Lemma

5.3
, we have

\begin{split}\Pr_{(h_{1},\dots,h_{K})\sim(X_{1},\dots,X_{K})}&\left[\forall i\in[K],\forall x\in[m],h_{i}(x)\notin P_{u,v}\right]\\
&\leq\beta\cdot(\beta+\alpha\cdot(1-\beta))^{K-1}\\
&\leq\beta\cdot(\beta+2\beta\cdot(1-\beta))^{K-1}\leq(3\beta)^{K}\\
\end{split}

In the analysis above, we use
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{(6+\varepsilon)\log(n)}
interchangeably with the
K
hash functions it visits in the random walk over
D
.

Choosing
K=\frac{\left(2+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)\log(n)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{3\beta}\right)}
, the probability that
P_{u,v}
intersects with
U_{\sigma}
is at most
\frac{1}{n^{2+\varepsilon/8}}
. Next, we apply a union bound over all pairs of vertices
u,v\in V
, for which
u
is connected to
v
by a
\lambda^{\prime}
-length path. Since there at most
n^{2}
such pairs, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{\varepsilon/8}}
over the choice of
\sigma
, for each such
u,v
, there exists a walk
\rho_{u,v}
that visits
\lambda^{\prime}-1
distinct vertices, whose vertex set is hit by
U_{\sigma}
, and thus, with this probability,
U_{\sigma}
is a
\lambda/2
-representative set for
G
.
∎

To prove Item (1), we store the seed
\sigma
on the work tape of size
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
. On input
i\in[|U_{\sigma}|]
in binary,
\mathcal{U}
first computes
a=\lfloor i/m\rfloor
and
b=i\pmod{m}
, followed by which it outputs
h_{a}(b)
. From
Proposition

2.9
, both operations can be performed in
\mathsf{poly}(\log(n))
time and
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
space. For the latter computation,
\mathcal{U}
obtains
h_{1}
from the first
2\log(n)
bits in
\sigma
, and starts a walk on
D
, through the remaining bits on
\sigma
. Using the explicitness of
D
from
Lemma

5.1
to obtain the
a^{\text{th}}
hash function from the random walk,
h_{a}(b)
can now be computed using the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
. From these lemmas, each of these operations can be done in
\mathsf{poly}(\log(n))
time and
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
space, and thus Item (1) follows.

Finally, we prove Item (2) of the theorem. Pick any
u,v\in V
that is connected by a path
\rho_{u,v}=(u,p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell^{\prime}},v)
. Now, consider a partition of
\rho_{u,v}
into subsequences, where
\rho^{1}
is the subpath of
\rho_{u,v}
of length
\lambda/2
from
u
to
q_{\lambda/2}
, and each subsequent
\rho^{i}
is the subpath of
\rho_{u,v}
from
q_{(i-1)\lambda/2}
to
q_{i\lambda/2}
visiting the next
\lambda/2
vertices that appear after
q_{(i-1)\lambda/2}
. Finally,
\rho^{\ell+1}
contains at most
\lambda/2
vertices that appear from
q_{\ell\lambda/2}
to
v
in
\rho_{u,v}
.

With high probability over
\sigma
,
U_{\sigma}
is a
\lambda/2
-representative set, and so, for each
i
, there exists a walk
\tilde{\rho}^{i}
from
q_{(i-1)\lambda/2}
to
q_{i\lambda/2}
that visits
(\lambda/2)-1
distinct vertices (which could even be
\rho^{i}
), whose vertex set
P^{i}
(that includes
u
) is hit by
U_{\sigma}
. For each
i
, pick an
s_{i}\in U_{\sigma}
that intersects
P^{i}
.

Now, consider the sequence
(u,s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell},v)
. For each pair
(s_{i},s_{i+1})
, there exists a walk from
s_{i}
to
q_{i\lambda/2}
in
\tilde{\rho}^{i}
that visits at most
(\lambda/2)-1
distinct vertices, which in turn implies a path of length at most
\lambda/2
between them. Similarly, we obtain a path from
q_{i\lambda}
to
s_{i+1}
in
\tilde{\rho}^{i+1}
of length at most
\lambda/2
. Together, there exists a path of length at most
\lambda
from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
. In addition, observe that the path from
u
to
s_{1}
has length at most
\lambda/2
, and the one from
s_{\ell\lambda/2}
to
v
has length at most
\lambda
, as the final set
P^{\ell+1}
might not be large enough to be hit by
U_{\sigma}
.
∎

6
Edit Distance, Longest Common Subsequence and Discrete Fréchet Distance

In this section, we focus on computing edit distance, longest common subsequence and discrete Fréchet distance. We prove the next two theorems.

Theorem 6.1
.

The length of the longest common subsequence and edit distance of two strings of length
n
can be computed in time
\mathsf{poly}(n)
, using
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
workspace and
\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}
catalytic space.

Theorem 6.2
.

Discrete Fréchet distance of two point sequences of length
n
with distance oracle whose values are in the range
\{0,\ldots,\mathsf{poly}(n)\}
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{4+\varepsilon})
, using
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
workspace and
\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}
catalytic space.

All of these results follow from the same procedure for computing (weighted) reachability on a directed grid graph. This procedure is presented in the next section.

6.1
Grid graphs reachability

For the remainder of this section, we consider only fields of the form
\mathbb{F}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
for some prime
p
.

For the purpose of our algorithm, we extend the grid-like graphs used for computing the metrics to be layered, denoted by
\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n}
.
Formally, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define
\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n}
as a directed graph such that

\displaystyle V(\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n})=

\displaystyle\,\{(i,j)\colon 0\leq i\leq n,0\leq j\leq n\},

\displaystyle E(\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n})=

\displaystyle\,\{((i,j),(i+1,j))\colon 0\leq i\leq n-1,0\leq j\leq n\}\,\cup

\displaystyle\,\{((i,j),(i+1,j+1))\colon 0\leq i\leq n-1,0\leq j\leq n-1\}\,\cup

\displaystyle\,\{((i,j),(i+1,j-1))\colon 0\leq i\leq n-1,1\leq j\leq n\}.

Given a directed acyclic layered graph
G=(V,E)
with weighted edges
\mu:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, we recall that the weight of an
s,t
-walk
W=(s=v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}=t)
is
\mu(W)=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\mu(v_{i},v_{i+1})
.
In fact, as we consider only layered graphs, and in particular
\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n}
, all walks from any
u
to any
v
are paths.
The restriction to paths will not cause any problems in the following algorithms, as in layered graphs, all paths between two vertices have the same length and recursively splitting the problem with respect to the layers decomposes the problem into disjoint subproblems.

We thus define the total path weight
10
10

10

for a particular
\ell
in Definition
3.1
with

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\tau_{in}\hfil

set to be the unit vector corresponding to
s
.
from
s
to
t
as
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}=\sum_{P\,s,t\text{-path}}\mu(P)
.
In line with the flow-based algorithm of
[
45
]
, our goal is to catalytically compute the total path weight from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in a weighted
\mathsf{Grid}
.
We make use of two catalytic subroutines, which we call
\mathsf{{INNER}}
and
\mathsf{{OUTER}}
.
To save space, we split each layer of the graph into a few color classes (which will be intervals in each of the layers), and then compute the total path weights recursively on the layers within the color classes up to the base case, where we will use only a single color class on which we will recurse slightly differently.
The former recursive computation is the
\mathsf{{OUTER}}
procedure, the latter is the
\mathsf{{INNER}}
procedure. We first start with describing the
\mathsf{{INNER}}
procedure.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists a catalytic subroutine
\mathsf{{INNER}}^{\ell}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern are the vertices with indices

.

This procedure uses
4^{\ell-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h)
weight oracle queries,
4^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))+5\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}4^{i}\leq 4^{\ell}\cdot(\mathcal{O}(h\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))+20)
time,
(\ell-1)\cdot h\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|)
catalytic space (not including the input registers), and
(\ell-1)\cdot 4+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)
free space.

Proof.

For simplicity of exposition, we assume that
n,h,\ell_{out}-\ell_{in}
are all powers of two. (This can be removed at the cost of a few bits of free space per level of recursion.)

We define the procedure recursively.
For
\ell=0
, we want to compute the flow path for two neighboring layers.
We can do this computation as follows: for each vertex
(\ell_{in},i)
in the input layer and each of its at most three neighbors
(\ell_{out},j)
in the output layer, we add
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}[j]\mathrel{+}=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern using a single call to the oracle
\mathcal{D}
, free space
\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|)
(which can be reused for each pair) and time
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))
per pair (the time comes from accessing the entry of the vector according to our methodology in the appendix). The catalytic space used is already provided as input, so we do not use any more.
Thus, as there are
\mathcal{O}(h)
edges we must iterate over, we use time
\mathcal{O}(h\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))
,
\mathcal{O}(h)
calls to the oracle and free space
\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)
, where the second term is the counter required for us to iterate over all the edges.
All of catalytic space used is provided as part of the input.

We use
V_{in},V_{mid},V_{out}
to refer to the sets of vertices corresponding to the respective layered color classes.
We also note that as
\mathsf{{INNER}}^{\ell-1}
is a catalytic subroutine, we can also use its inverse
11
11

11

As in previous statements, we may run the same program backwards while reverting the instructions dealing with catalytic space. We also need to take care of the parameters
\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid}
, but we can use identical ideas from the original algorithm to do so.
.
We are now ready to define

9.

\ell_{out}=2\cdot\ell_{mid}-\ell_{in}

By induction, we require
4\cdot(4^{\ell-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))+5\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}4^{i})+5=4^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))+5\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}4^{i}
time.

We only require 4 bits of new free space within the recursion for the instruction counter. Otherwise, we only use the previously stored
\ell
s, therefore the free space required remains
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
for the input parameters and by induction,
4+(\ell-2)\cdot 4+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)=(\ell-1)\cdot 4+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)
.
We use a new part of the catalytic space, so using induction, we use
h\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|)+(\ell-2)\cdot h\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|)
, and thus the total catalytic space used is
(\ell-1)\cdot h\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|)
plus the input registers.
Finally, the number of weight oracle queries is again by induction
4\cdot 4^{\ell-2}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h)=4^{\ell-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h)
.

The correctness of the computation adding the weighted path-flow follows almost identically to the proof of
Lemma

3.4
.
We therefore only focus on ensuring that the layer registers
\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid}
have correct values so that we recurse correctly while using only
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
space.
Our invariant will be that after returning from a recursive call
\mathsf{{INNER}}^{\ell}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}},\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}};\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid};i_{top},h)
, the two variables
\ell_{in}
and
\ell_{out}
will have their values identical to the values at the start of the procedure.
In the case when
\ell=0
, we never change these values, so the invariant holds.

Next, we consider
\ell\geq 1
.
The first operation only computes the index of the middle layer.
After every even operation, we use
\ell_{mid}
and one of
\ell_{in},\ell_{out}
, as the in/out layers for the recursive call, while the other
\ell_{b}
is used as the mid-layer.
Therefore, the induction hypothesis guarantees that
\ell_{mid}
and one of the other two will be returned with the values set as they were when the subroutine was called.
In the following instructions, we then reconstruct the value that may have been changed so that all three variables have the same values as at the start of the procedure.
∎

We also note that we do not optimize the number of catalytic vectors.
Indeed, one can only use three vectors and swap them around similarly to
\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid}
.

Definition 6.4

(Layered color classes)
.

Let
C
be a parameter describing the number of color classes.
Consider
V(\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n})=\{(i,j)\colon 0\leq i\leq n,0\leq j\leq n\}
.
We define the
layer
of each vertex as its first coordinate.
We further define
\chi:V\rightarrow[C]
and
\delta:V\rightarrow[n/C]
as
\chi((i,j))=\lceil(j+1)/(n/C)\rceil
and
\delta((i,j))=j\bmod(n/C)
as the color class and index within the color class respectively.

We note that if two vertices share the second coordinate, their color classes are always identical.

Figure 1:
An example of
\mathsf{Grid}_{5,5}
with three color classes.

In the base case of the outer algorithm, we consider only a small subgraph of the grid graph.
However, as paths between two vertices of the subgraph do not have to be contained in the subgraph itself (see Figure
2
), we must consider a slightly larger subgraph, in particular the single color class becomes larger by a multiplicative factor.
In order to ensure that we really only add the paths between the original smaller color classes, we set the weight of all edges from the first and last layer of the graph that are not incident to vertices of the smaller color class.
This new oracle is then used for the inner algorithm and also for the final algorithm to ensure we count only relevant paths.

Figure 2:
The larger red vertices are a part of the induced rectangle, but the bold red path connecting them leaves the induced rectangle.

\cdots

Figure 3:
The behavior of the masking oracle in
Lemma

6.5
. The dashed edges have their edge weights set to
0
by the mask.

Lemma 6.5

(Weight Oracle for
\mathsf{{OUTER}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a weight oracle for the graph
\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n}
with edge weights
\mu:E(\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.
Consider an induced subgraph
H=H(\ell_{in},\ell_{out},i_{top},h,i_{in},i_{out},h_{class})
of
\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n}
such that
V(H)=\{(i,j):\ell_{in}\leq i\leq\ell_{out},i_{top}\leq j<i_{top}+h\}
with edge weights
\mu^{\prime}:V(H)\times V(H)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that

\mu^{\prime}((i,j),(i+1,k))=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }(i=\ell_{in}\land(j<i_{in}\lor i_{in}+h_{class}\leq j))\,\lor\\
&\phantom{\text{if }}(i+1=\ell_{out}\land(k<i_{out}\lor i_{out}+h_{class}\leq k)),\\
\mu((i,j),(i+1,k))&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then there exists a weight oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for the graph
H(\ell_{in},\ell_{out},i_{top},h,i_{in},i_{out},h_{class})
and edge weights
\mu^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
requires only additive
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
space and
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
time on top of the requirements for
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

The algorithm exactly follows the formula defining
\mu^{\prime}
– we first check the conditions and then we either return zero immediately, or we defer to the original oracle.
In either case, we only do constant number of comparisons and additions, and we need a constant number of variables for those, yielding our logarithmic space and time additive overhead.
∎

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\tau_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\tau_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}\cdot\mathcal{W}
with respect to the color class
c_{in}
in layer
\ell_{in}
and color class
c_{out}
in layer
\ell_{out}
.

This procedure uses free space
\ell\cdot(\log C+4)+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)
, catalytic space
\ell\cdot\frac{n}{C}\cdot\log|\mathbb{F}|+\mathcal{O}(n/C\cdot\log(n/C)\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|))
, time
(4C)^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{3})+5\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(4C)^{i}
and
(4C)^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}((\frac{n}{C})^{3})
oracle queries.

Proof.

As in
Lemma

6.3
, we assume that
n,h,\ell_{out}-\ell_{in}
are all powers of two. (This can be removed at the cost of a few bits of free space per level of recursion.)

We prove this using induction.
For
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{0}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}},\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern , we want to apply
Lemma

6.3
using
\mathsf{{INNER}}^{\log(n/C)}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r^{\prime}_{out}\hfil$\crcr}}};\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid};i_{top},h)
.

First, we note that if
|c_{in}-c_{out}|\geq 2
, we do not need to do anything, as having two color classes whose indices differ by at least two means that any path between the two vertices must change height by one per each step, and there are only
n/C
possible steps, while there are at least
n/C+2
steps needed, and thus no path between these layers can exist. Therefore, in such case, the subroutine returns immediately with no changes.

If
|c_{in}-c_{out}|\leq 1
, then we need to consider the subgrid containing the all paths from the left color class
c_{in}
to the right color class
c_{out}
.
However, we might need to consider more than the two subgrids containing these two color classes to cover all possible paths, as some path could jump above or below one of the color classes and then return back into the subgrid as shown in Figure
2
.
To cover the subgrid above, we set
i_{top}:=\max(\min(c_{in},c_{out})-1,0)\cdot n/C
and
h:=4n/C
to preserve our assumption on powers of two.
Using the notation from
Lemma

6.5
, we now focus on the subgraph
H(\ell_{in},\ell_{out},i_{top},h,c_{in}\cdot n/C,c_{out}\cdot n/C,n/C)
.
The description of the subgraph comprises the seven
\log(n)
-bit integers.
We pass these as parameters together with the oracle
\mathcal{D}
to the oracle from
Lemma

6.5
, and so we obtain an oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
with only additive logarithmic overhead.
We then allocate the two catalytic register vectors of size
4n/C
and
swap
the currently used

.

\hfil\textstyle r^{\prime}_{in}\hfil

does not change, and the only region with changes in

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r^{\prime}_{out}\hfil

is the region that is swapped with

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r_{out}\hfil

, and thus after the final swap,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle r^{\prime}_{out}\hfil

remains unchanged after all operations.

Analyzing time, we need
4^{\log(n/C)}\cdot(\mathcal{O}(\frac{n}{C}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))+20)=\mathcal{O}((\frac{n}{C})^{3}\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(|\mathbb{F}|)))
steps.
The required free space is
(\log(n/C)-1)\cdot 4+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)=\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)
, and we use
(\ell-1)\cdot h\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|)=\mathcal{O}(n/C\cdot\log(n/C)\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|))
catalytic space.
Finally, we need
4^{\ell-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}(h)=\mathcal{O}((\frac{n}{C})^{3})
weight oracle queries.

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}[v_{in}]\cdot\mathcal{W}_{v_{in},v_{mid}}\forall v_{mid}

9.

\ell_{out}=2\cdot\ell_{mid}-\ell_{in}

The total time spent is by induction
4C\cdot((4C)^{\ell-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{3})+5\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-2}(4C)^{i})+5=(4C)^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{3})+5\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(4C)^{i}
, while we use
4C\cdot((4C)^{\ell-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}((\frac{n}{C})^{3}))=(4C)^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}((\frac{n}{C})^{3})
weight oracle queries.

Regarding free space used, we only need a variable for iterating over all
C
color classes which requires
\log C
space, and 4 bits for instruction count.
Thus, using induction, we require space
\log C+4+(\ell-1)\cdot(\log C+4)+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)=\ell\cdot(\log C+4)+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)
.
For catalytic space, we allocate a new field vector that requires
\frac{n}{C}\cdot\log|\mathbb{F}|
bits, and by induction, we require
\frac{n}{C}\cdot\log|\mathbb{F}|+(\ell-1)\cdot\frac{n}{C}\cdot\log|\mathbb{F}|+\mathcal{O}(n/C\cdot\log(n/C)\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|))=\ell\cdot\frac{n}{C}\cdot\log|\mathbb{F}|+\mathcal{O}(n/C\cdot\log(n/C)\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|))
.

The correctness of the computation follows identical ideas from
Lemma

3.4
, while proving that we maintain the correct values of
\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid}
is identical to the proof in
Lemma

6.3
.
∎

We now have all the tools necessary to state and prove the main result of this section: we can compute the sum of all weights of
s,t
-paths modulo a prime
p
for two particular vertices
s,t
.
Our approach will use the path-flow propagation of
Lemma

6.6
with the ideas of
[
45
]
so that we are able to extract the desired value using four runs of the algorithm.

Theorem 6.7
.

There exists a catalytic algorithm that, given a prime
p=\mathsf{poly}(n)
, a graph
\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n}
with weights
\mu:E(\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
given as an oracle
\mathcal{D}
that returns the weights modulo
p
, and two vertices
u,v\in V(\mathsf{Grid}_{n,n})
described by their indices in the layer such that the distance between
u
and
v
is a power of two, computes the value
\mathcal{W}_{u,v}=\sum_{P\,u,v\text{-path}}\mu(P)\bmod{p}
.

The algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3+\varepsilon})
using free space
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
, catalytic space
\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}
and
\mathcal{O}(\frac{n^{3+\varepsilon}}{2^{3\sqrt{\log n}}})
weight oracle queries.

Proof.

Our goal is to use the
\mathsf{{OUTER}}
procedure from
Lemma

6.6
.

To do so, we need the following: a suitable choice of the parameter
C
, two catalytic vector registers of length
n/C
, three
\log(n)
-bit free space variables and two more
\log(C)
-bit variables for the description of the first and last color class.

We set
C:=2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil}
and allocate the two register vectors
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle r_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern according to Remark
A
.
Let
\ell_{u},i_{u}
and
\ell_{v},i_{v}
be the indices of
u
and
v
respectively.
We can easily compute
\ell_{v}-\ell_{u}
, from which we can compute a suitable
\ell
to use the right level of recursion for
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
.
Similarly, we allocate the three
\log(n)
-bit integers for layer counting
\ell_{in},\ell_{out},\ell_{mid}
, where
\ell_{in}:=\ell_{u}
and
\ell_{out}:=\ell_{v}
.
The value of
\ell_{mid}
may be arbitrary as it is immediately replaced in the procedure.
We also allocate the two values
c_{in},c_{out}
for the color classes of
u
and
v
respectively.

Finally, we also use
Lemma

6.5
on the subgraph
H(\ell_{in},\ell_{out},0,n,i_{u},i_{v},1)
to obtain an oracle
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
which sets the weight zero to every edge coming from the layer
\ell_{u}
unless it is incident with
u
, and to every edge coming to the layer
\ell_{v}
unless it is incident with
v
, and it mirrors the original oracle
\mathcal{D}
otherwise.

As observed in the proof of
Lemma

6.6
, we can also run the
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
procedure in reverse with flipped signs to subtract the values.
Therefore, we perform the following procedure:

To argue correctness, we need to show that the catalytic space has been restored to the original state and that the value returned is indeed correct.
