0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\tau_{in}\hfil$\crcr}}}+1)\cdot\mathcal{W}_{u,v}
.
Thus,
r_{2}-r_{1}=\mathcal{W}_{u,v}
, and performing all the arithmetic modulo
p
ensures that we indeed return
\mathcal{W}_{u,v}\bmod p
.

We now analyze time and space complexity.
We split the analysis into two steps: first, we consider the resources needed for
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
.
In the worst case, the depth necessary for
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
to work is
\ell=\lceil\sqrt{\log n}\rceil
.
Then,
Lemma

6.6
gives us that
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
uses free space
\ell\cdot(\log C+4)+\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathbb{F}|+\log n)=\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil\cdot(\log(2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil})+4)+\mathcal{O}(\log p+\log n)=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
.
The catalytic space used is
\ell\cdot\frac{n}{C}\cdot\log|\mathbb{F}|+\mathcal{O}(n/C\cdot\log(n/C)\cdot\log(|\mathbb{F}|))=\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil\cdot\frac{n}{2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil\cdot}}\cdot\log n+\mathcal{O}(\frac{n}{2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil\cdot}}\cdot\log(\frac{n}{2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil\cdot}})\cdot\log n)=\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}
.
The total time spent is
(4C)^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{3})+5\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(4C)^{i}\leq(4\cdot 2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil})^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{3})+5\cdot(4\cdot 2^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil})^{\lceil\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}\rceil}=\mathcal{O}(n^{3+\varepsilon})
plus we use
(4C)^{\ell}\cdot\mathcal{O}((\frac{n}{C})^{3})=\mathcal{O}(\frac{n^{3+\varepsilon}}{2^{3\sqrt{\log n}}})
oracle queries.

Regarding the rest of the program, we need to allocate a constant number of
\mathcal{O}(\log(n))
-bit free space variables, and two vectors of
\mathcal{O}(\log(p))
-bit field elements of length
n/C=\frac{n}{2^{\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}}}
in the catalytic space.

Thus, the total free space usage is
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
.
We also use
\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}+\frac{n}{2^{\sqrt{(\varepsilon/2.1)\log n}}}\cdot\log(p)=\frac{n}{2^{\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})}}
catalytic space.
The running time is
4\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{3+\varepsilon})+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\log(n)))=\mathcal{O}(n^{3+\varepsilon})
, as each of the lines 2, 4, 6, 8, 9 requires logarithmic time in the worst case and the other lines are covered by the time complexity of
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
.
We only use oracle queries in
\mathsf{{OUTER}}^{\ell}
, so the number of weight oracle queries is
\mathcal{O}(\frac{n^{3+\varepsilon}}{2^{3\sqrt{\log n}}})
.
∎

We remark that the assumption on the distance between
u
and
v
being a power of two is only done for simplicity of exposition.
It is possible to support arbitrary distances at the cost of
\mathcal{O}(1)
bits of free space per level of recursion, which would amount only to
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
additional free space required.

6.2
Computing edit distance, LCS and discrete Fréchet distance

Our goal is now to establish Theorem
6.1
and then Theorem
6.2
.
To compute edit distance and longest common subsequence we will need to calculate a certain
N
-bit number
x
where
N=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.
We do not have enough space to store
x
or the intermediate results so we cannot compute it directly.
Instead we will compute
x
modulo various
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-bit primes, i.e. in Chinese remainder representation,
and then access individual bits of
x

using the algorithm of
[
56
]
from Proposition
2.11
.

Next we will explain how to compute edit distance of two strings using Theorem
6.7
.
We will reduce computation of edit distance to computing a total path weight value in a particular grid graph.
From the binary representation of the total path weight value we can determine the edit distance of the two strings.
The grid graph will be a simple modification of the usual edit distance graph.
We can compute the total path weight value in the grid graph modulo any
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-bit prime using Theorem
6.7
.
Using Proposition
2.11
we can recover the binary representation of the total path weight value and hence compute the edit distance of the two strings.

\mathsf{ED}_{n,n}
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
\leadsto

Figure 4:
The transformation from
\mathsf{ED}_{n,n}
into
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
. The source and sink vertices of
\mathsf{ED}_{n,n}
and
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
which correspond to each other are shown in the same color. There are no edges in
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
outside the dashed square.

For two strings
x
and
y
of length
n
, define the edit distance graph
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
to have vertex set
\{0,\dots,n\}\times\{0,\dots,n\}
,
horizontal edges
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j)
of cost 1, for
0\leq i<n
and
0\leq j\leq n
,
vertical edges
(i,j)\rightarrow(i,j+1)
of cost 1, for
0\leq i\leq n
and
0\leq j<n
,
and diagonal edges
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j+1)
, for
0\leq i,j<n
,
where the cost of the edge is 0 if
x_{i+1}=y_{j+1}
and it is 1 otherwise.

It is well known that the edit distance of
x
and
y
is the minimum cost of a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
(see e.g.
[
16
,
37
]
).
(Here by the cost of a path we understand the sum of the costs of the edges on the path as opposed to the weight of a path which is the product of the weights.)
We will embed
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
into
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
so that the minimum cost of a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}

can be determined from a particular total path weight in
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
.
To embed
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
into
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
we will rotate the graph by 45 degrees clockwise (Fig.
4
)
and split each diagonal edge into two by adding an auxiliary vertex.

We will define weights of the edges in
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
as follows.
Each vertex
(i,j)\in\{0,\dots,2n\}^{2}
of
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
, where
i=j+1\pmod{2}
, corresponds to an auxiliary diagonal vertex,
and its only outgoing edge
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j)
has weight 1; the other two out-going edges have weight 0.
For each vertex
(i,j)\in\{0,\dots,2n\}^{2}
where
i=j\pmod{2}
we let
i^{\prime}=(i-j+n)/2
and
j^{\prime}=(i+j-n)/2
.
If
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\{0,\dots,n\}\times\{0,\dots,n\}
then the vertex
(i,j)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
corresponds to a vertex
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
.
We set weights of edges leaving
(i,j)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
as follows:

1.

If
0\leq i^{\prime}<n
then the edge
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j-1)
has weight
16^{n}
.

2.

If
0\leq j^{\prime}<n
then the edge
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j+1)
has weight
16^{n}
.

3.

Finally, if
0\leq i^{\prime},j^{\prime}<n
then the edge
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j)
has weight
1
if
x_{i^{\prime}+1}=y_{j^{\prime}+1}
, and weight
16^{n}
otherwise.

The weight of all other edges in
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
is set to zero.
We call
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
with this particular weighting scheme
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
.

Hence, vertex
(0,0)
of
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
corresponds to the vertex
(0,n)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
,
and vertex
(n,n)
corresponds to the vertex
(2n,n)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between paths from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
and paths from
(0,n)
to
(2n,n)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
that go only through non-zero-weight edges.
A path of cost
w
from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
corresponds to a path of weight
16^{nw}
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
.
(A cost-one edge in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
corresponds to an edge with weight
16^{n}
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
and a cost-zero edge in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
corresponds to an edge of weight
1
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
.
The
auxiliary
diagonal edges of
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
have weight 1 in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
so they don’t affect the product of weights.)

Given
n
and
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-bit prime
p
in binary, we can compute
16^{n}\bmod p
in
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-space by repeated squaring and multiplications.
Hence, it is straightforward to implement a weight oracle
\mathcal{D}
for
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
as required by Theorem
6.7
.
The oracle will use space
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
for its internal computation and it will need an access to the two input strings
x
and
y
.
If the length of the two strings is not a power of two the oracle can extend the graph
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
by extra vertices
to make it of desired size
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n^{\prime},2n^{\prime}}
(where
n<n^{\prime}<2n
is a power of two)
where there will be a path of weight 1 leading from the original target vertex
(2n,n)
to the new target vertex
(2n^{\prime},n)
,
and all other new edges will be assigned weight 0.

Notice, there are at most
3^{2n}=9^{n}
paths from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
as each vertex has out-degree at most 3 and each path is of length at most
2n
.
Hence, there are at most
9^{n}<16^{n}
paths of non-zero weight from
(0,n)
to
(2n,n)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
.
For
w\in\{0,\dots,n\}
, let
n_{w}
denote the number of paths of cost
w
from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
.
The total path weight from
s=(0,n)
to
t=(2n,n)
in
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}

corresponds to
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}=\sum_{w=0}^{2n}16^{nw}\cdot n_{w}
.
Since each
n_{w}<16^{n}
, if we represent
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
in base
16^{n}
each digit will correspond to one of
n_{w}
’s.
Furthermore, if
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
is represented in binary then bits
4n(w+1)-1,\dots,4nw
represent
n_{w}
in binary
(where the lowest order bit has index 0).
Thus, if we determine
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
we can determine the first non-zero
n_{w}
for
w=0,\dots,2n
by inspecting the binary representation of
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
.
(This requires
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-bits of additional space.)
The binary representation of
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
has
N=(2n+1)4n=8n^{2}+4n
bits.
We can access the individual bits of
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
using Proposition
2.11
that will make at most
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})=\mathcal{O}(n^{4})
queries to
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}\bmod p_{i}
,
i<N
,
where each of the queries can be answered by running the algorithm from Theorem
6.7
on our
\mathsf{Grid}_{x,y}
.
Hence, we can compute the edit distance of
x
and
y
.

Overall the algorithm requires only
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-space in addition to the space needed by the algorithm of Theorem
6.7
to answer
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}\bmod p_{i}
queries.
This establishes Theorem
6.1
for edit distance.

We note that we do not establish the precise time complexity, as it also depends on the time complexity of
Proposition

2.11
which we do not attempt to optimize here.

The same approach also works for the weighted versions of edit distance if the weights are
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
-bit integers with appropriate changes to the weighting scheme.
The number of bits in the number would then be
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\cdot w_{\text{max}})
, where
w_{\text{max}}=\mathsf{poly}(n)
is the largest possible weight, which would still yield a polynomial time algorithm.

Longest common subsequence of two strings can be computed in a similar manner.
Instead of the graph
\mathsf{ED}_{x,y}
one considers an almost identical graph
\mathsf{LCS}_{x,y}
where horizontal and vertical edges have cost zero
and each diagonal edge
(i,j)\rightarrow(i+1,j+1)
has cost one if
x_{i+1}=y_{j+1}
and cost zero otherwise.
The length of the longest common subsequence of
x
and
y
corresponds to the maximum cost of a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
.
Similarly to the edit distance we embed
\mathsf{LCS}_{x,y}
into
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
, and compute the value of the total path weight
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
, for
s=(0,n)
to
t=(2n,n)
.
From the binary representation of
\mathcal{W}_{s,t}
we can determine the maximum cost of a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
that appears in the original graph
\mathsf{LCS}_{x,y}
.
This implies Theorem
6.1
for longest common subsequence.

Computing the discrete Fréchet distance of two point sequences
x
and
y
can be done using a similar technique, while using a well-known reduction that is already implicit in the algorithm by Eiter and Mannila
[
51
]
.
By our assumption on the distance oracle, we know that all the pairwise distances of points from the two sequences are from
\{0,\dots,\mathsf{poly}(n)\}
.
For
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathsf{poly}(n)\}
we can define a graph
\mathsf{Frech}_{x,y,m}
on a vertex set
\{0,\dots,n\}\times\{0,\dots,n\}

with horizontal, vertical and diagonal edges like in the edit distance graph
except that we remove edges leading into each vertex
(i,j)
where the distance between
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
is more than
m
.
Such vertices will become unreachable.
The cost of all the remaining edges will be one.
In this graph
\mathsf{Frech}_{x,y,m}
, there is a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
if and only if the discrete Fréchet distance of
x
and
y
is at most
m
.
Again we can embed the graph into
\mathsf{Grid}_{2n,2n}
while assigning weight 0 to missing edges and weight one to edges that are present.
We can use the algorithm from Theorem
6.7
to determine the total path weight from
(0,n)
into
(2n,n)
modulo a prime.
As all paths will have weight one the total path weight counts the number of paths from
(0,n)
into
(2n,n)
.
To determine whether the discrete Fréchet distance of
x
and
y
is at most
m
we just have to check whether the total path weight is non-zero.
The total path weight is zero if and only if its value is zero modulo the first
4n
primes.
Thus, we can check whether there is a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
in
\mathsf{Frech}_{x,y,m}
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{4+\varepsilon})
.

Using
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
iterations of the algorithm, we perform binary search on
m
from
\{0,\ldots,\mathsf{poly}(n)\}
to find the smallest
m
with the property that the discrete Fréchet distance of
x
and
y
is at most
m
.
This is the discrete Fréchet distance of
x
and
y
.
We conclude Theorem
6.2
.

Appendix A
Catalytic Technicalities

In this section, we deal with the remaining technical details of our catalytic algorithms.

Catalytic vectors
\bmod

p
.

Our algorithm views pieces of its catalytic memory as vectors of registers
r_{1},\dots,r_{m}
storing values from
\{0,\dots,p-1\}
.
If we allocate
w=\lceil\log p\rceil
bits to each of the registers in a naive way
the initial value in some of the registers could be out of the allowed range when
p
is not a power of two.
Previous works
[
34
,
40
]
addressed this issue using techniques which lead to large time overhead when accessing individual registers.
Here we present a different technique which builds on the previous ones which is suitable for machines that have random access to the catalytic tape.
Our technique incurs
\mathcal{O}(\log n\log p)
overhead to access a register given by its index.

Our solution will use
8mw
bits of the catalytic tape to store the registers.
It has three phases: pre-processing phase taking time
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(m+p)))
, working phase when we can work with the registers,
and post-processing phase taking time
\mathcal{O}(m\cdot\mathsf{poly}(\log(m+p)))
which returns the catalytic tape to its original state
(assuming all the registers are already returned to the same value as they had at the beginning of the working phase.)

Assume that
m
is a power of two.
Choose some constant
C\geq 1
, set
M=m^{C}
.
The registers are allocated as follows.
We will divide the allocated space into groups of
b=8\log M
cells, each cell having
w
bits.
The
i
-th group of cells,
i=1,\dots,m/\log M
, will accommodate
\log M
registers
r_{1+(i-1)\log M},\dots,r_{i\log M}
.
Within the group, the
j
-th register will use the
j
-th cell that contains a value in range
\{0,\dots,p-1\}
.
Hence, depending on the exact details of the computational model locating the cell storing a given register takes time at most
\mathcal{O}(\log M\log p)
to inspect cells in a given group.

The only issue is that initially a group might contain fewer than
\log M
cells with a value in the range
\{0,\dots,p-1\}
.
(Cells with a value in that range keep the value in that range as register cannot be assigned a value outside of its range.)
We call such groups
unhealthy
.

Unhealthy groups will be modified during the pre-processing stage so that they will be usable in the working stage.
During the pre-processing stage we will create a linked list of the unhealthy cells
so that during the post-processing phase we will be able to return them to their original contents.
The pointers of the linked list will use space obtained by compressing each unhealthy group so only
\mathcal{O}(\log m)
extra bits will be needed to store the pointer to the first unhealthy group.

During the pre-processing phase if a group is unhealthy, then all but at most
b/8
cells in the group have the most significant bit set to one.
We will flip the most significant bit of the first
b/4
cells in the group.
Since
b/4-b/8\geq\log M
the group is now suitable to accommodate
\log M
registers within its first
b/4
cells.
The latter
3b/4
cells will be compressed by removing their most significant bit which will free
3b/4
bits.
The
3b/4
bits will be used to store the pointer to the next unhealthy group (
\log M
bits)
and information about the deleted most significant bits (
5b/8
bits).

The most significant bits are encoded as triples: for each triple we have one indicator bit saying whether it consists of only 1’s
and if not we specify the whole triple.
The indicators occupy
b/4
bits and the explicit triples take at most
3b/8
bits.
Together this is at most
5b/8
bits so there are at least
3b/4-5b/8=b/8=C\log m\geq\log m
bits available for the pointer.

In the post-processing phase we go through the list of unhealthy groups and undo the compression and the bit flips.

Notice, the unhealthy groups provide extra unused space when
C>1
.
This extra space can be used if we need to manage multiple vectors in the catalytic memory at once without incurring overhead
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
bits
for a separate pointer to unhealthy cell list in each of the vectors.

The above construction uses
8mw
bits to store about
mw
bits of information.
One can easily improve the construction to require space only
(2+\epsilon)nw

by a better compression of unhealthy groups and by making the groups constant time larger.
This would affect the time of pre- and post-processing by poly-logarithmic factors and the access time by a constant factor.
